# Project's Expected Output 3: GLOF and flash flood risk and impact reduction measures implemented in priority glacial lake watersheds.

## Activities

### Constitution of GLOF and Flash Flood Risk Reduction Committees

**GDDI activities**

1. Organize inclusive consultations and workshops to identify and prioritize GLOF and flash flood risks.
2. Conduct participatory vulnerability assessments and risk management workshops to enhance understanding, awareness, and incorporation of gender perspectives in the planning and implementation of GLOF and flash flood risk reduction measures.

**Baseline**

Limited women's participation in decision-making processes at the local level and poor understanding of GLOF and flash flood risks.

**Target**

More than 45% of participants are women from highly vulnerable communities who actively participate in workshops and community meetings to enhance their understanding of GLOF and flash flood risks, and to develop and implement risk reduction measures.

**GDDI outcome indicators**

Percentage of women participating in GLOF and flash flood risk management activities.

**Timeline**

Year 2-6

**Responsible**

Implementing partners (IPs) and PWO staff.

**Estimated cost**

USD 1,000,000 (including NDRRMAA)

---

### Outcomes expected for GDDI awareness and risk governance activities

1. GLOF and flash flood risks are identified and prioritized by women and men leaders on GLOF/EWS committees.
2. Gender-responsive policies and action plans are developed and implemented to address GLOF and flash flood risks.
3. Gender-responsive tools and approaches for GDDI data, and knowledge by IPs, community leaders, and local governments are developed and applied.

# Strength and Weakness of GEOSI

## Strengths
- 40% of GEOSI is a data collection system for CC-induced diseases.
- CC-induced diseases are a major cause of death worldwide.
- GEOSI has been used to establish GEOSI as a reliable source of information on CC-related diseases.

## Weaknesses
- GEOSI has not yet been fully implemented in all countries.
- The system is still in the early stages of development and testing.
- There are concerns about the accuracy and reliability of the data collected by GEOSI.

# Executive Summary

On his recent visit to the Government of Nepal (GoN), a top-year project proposal on "Protecting livelihoods and assets at Risk from Climate Change induced flooding in Global River basins of Nepal" was presented. The project aims to protect lives, livelihoods, and assets from climate-induced disasters by providing support from several other related mechanisms such as GEF/MUENR, MAF/DPIDAR, and others.

The project, titled "4C: Climate Change Adaptation for Protecting Livelihoods and Assets at Risk from Climate Change induced Flooding in Global River basins of Nepal", aims to provide global public goods by protecting livelihoods and assets at risk from climate change-induced flooding. The global river basins of Nepal are prone to frequent flooding due to the impact of climate change, which affects indigenous peoples (IPs), local communities, and vulnerable groups, such as women, youth, and children.

The project will focus on reducing disaster risks by implementing early warning systems, improving preparedness, and enhancing response mechanisms. It aims to support the Government of Nepal in achieving its goals under the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction (SFDRR) and the Paris Agreement. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). As part of the project, the GoN has committed to implementing various measures to reduce disaster risks and enhance resilience.

The project will be implemented in collaboration with several international organizations, including UNDP, CISCO, Private Sector, and Government institutions. The project aims to support the Government of Nepal in achieving its goals under the SFDRR and the Paris Agreement by providing technical assistance and capacity building to local communities.

The project will focus on reducing disaster risks by implementing early warning systems, improving preparedness, and enhancing response mechanisms. It aims to support the Government of Nepal in achieving its goals under the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction (SFDRR) and the Paris Agreement. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). As part of the project, the GoN has committed to implementing various measures to reduce disaster risks and enhance resilience.

The project will be implemented in collaboration with several international organizations, including UNDP, CISCO, Private Sector, and Government institutions. The project aims to support the Government of Nepal in achieving its goals under the SFDRR and the Paris Agreement by providing technical assistance and capacity building to local communities.

# Literature reviews of various studies in Nepal and elsewhere such as in Pakistan and Bhutan

Literature reviews of various studies in Nepal and elsewhere such as in Pakistan and Bhutan indicate that the prevalence of G6PD deficiency is high among the population. The level of awareness about G6PD deficiency is low, and its community-level knowledge is limited and often misinformed. Historical documentation indicates a lack of understanding of the disease.

This background paper outlines the data collection, analysis, report writing, and development of the project. It also highlights the importance of the Global Health Security Agenda (GHSA) and the One Health approach to address G6PD deficiency in Nepal.

The key were taken for the field level primary data collection. A total of 30 key informant interviews (KIIs), 9 focus group discussions (FGDs), and 188 household surveys (HHs) were conducted by 48 people from the field. The data was collected in the field works as organized following the standard operating procedures on the basis of the data collection tool, and generated input for the action plan, and its evaluation work plan to address G6PD deficiency.

## Key findings

The findings from desk reviews reveal that though there has been a limited effort made towards managing G6PD data since the 2015 Earthquake in Nepal, there is a lack of G6PD disaggregated data and only in terms of G6PD in general (female/female induced disorders).

Floods and landslides that occur every year caused higher rates of human loss and property in the affected areas. The floods and landslides cause the highest toll of lives lost due to G6PD deficiency that after few years. The floods and landslides caused the highest toll of lives lost due to G6PD deficiency that after few years. The floods and landslides caused the highest toll of lives lost due to G6PD deficiency that after few years.

The findings from field studies were among the most vulnerable of those affected by major events, such as floods and landslides. The findings from field studies were among the most vulnerable of those affected by major events, such as floods and landslides. 2. **Third World Women**

The critical area for intervention is the control of development and cultural change. The social and economic conditions of the Third World women must be improved through the integration of their traditional roles with modern technology and increased participation in decision-making processes. This has substantially increased poor and marginalized groups' income, particularly among women. The programs enable women to learn about their natural conditions in well-being and health. They are also given the opportunity to participate in community development activities and to improve their living standards. The programs also help to reduce the number of children born into poverty.

3. **Poor and Historically Excluded Women**

Poor and historically excluded women and men particularly living in geographically remote and disaster-prone areas, mainly between 25 to 50 years from the global labor force, are often neglected by the government and international organizations. The poor outreach mechanisms, exclusive and isolated from the mainstream society, are often ignored by the government and international organizations. The poor women's empowerment programs, including and organized groups, particularly with the domination by rural women, have been successful in improving their lives. For example, women in the local groups are strong, ultimately the policies and power relations and authorities on who should be included or represented or not.

4. **The Participation of Women**

Women and men in local development planning, disaster risk management and climate change related activities and decision-making are highly effective in promoting gender equality and equity. ### Institutional level findings:

1. The planning and decision-making processes seem to be largely controlled by the local government and nongovernmental organizations (NGOs) that have been established and funded by the government and development agencies.
2. The impact of changing climate together with the transition from the past governance system has affected the planning and decision-making process. A large number of women have come into leadership positions. However, women's leadership and ability to make and influence decisions are still limited due to lack of education and security concerns. Women are often excluded from decision-making processes at all levels.
3. There needs to be improvement in the national and local level institutions and the need to address GESI. However, knowledge and skills on social methods and approaches are very low among government officials, and there is no program or policy cycle in place.

10. The stakeholders and beneficiaries, particularly in cluster risk-related groups, committees, and activities are highly considered as a marginalized group; with this notion that disasters affect them more than others. However, the needs of women and marginalized groups are not specifically considered, which sometimes is almost the same across different categories.

### Recommendations:

1. Establish a working definition of the primary stakeholders (women, marginalized and highly vulnerable groups), and define the goals for the project area by conducting and organizing focus group discussions with the community members to understand their needs and interactions. For example: **Historical Excludes + Marginalized + Vulnerable**.
2. Monitor the advancement of social integration and risk, loss, and damage in this area to open up the discussion on how to improve the situation.
3. Ensure that the government and local authorities are aware of the issues and take action.

4. Increase poor and marginalized women and men's income and employment by investing in climate-smart agriculture, fisheries, forestry, and other livelihoods. Promote cooperatives, particularly women run cooperatives, and Micro-Enterprise (ME) loans are accessible to poor and vulnerable groups. 5. Ensure women, transgender/compte, and men's access to information, communication, and education on the poor, women and highly vulnerable groups on the impacts of climate change. Promote gender-responsive climate change education in schools and communities.

6. Support the Monitoring Information System (MIS) of the project to capture gender and climate change data and indicators. Ensure that these are disaggregated by sex and age. Use this information to identify challenges and learning, which should feed back into the project's annual planning and budgeting process. Ensure that the MIS is linked with the local government units' (LGUs) information systems on natural, physical and community-based resources.

# 1. Background

A five-year proposal on "Protecting Wetlands and Ascent at Risk from Climate Change Induced Flooding in Critical River Basins of Nepal" being funded by UNDP-Nepal in collaboration with Government of Nepal (GoN) has been submitted to the Government of Nepal. The proposal was developed jointly by the Department of National Parks and Wildlife Conservation (DNPWC), Ministry of Forests and Environment, and the Department of Hydrology and Meteorology (DHM)/Ministry of Water Supply, Energy and Sanitation. The GoN has requested the Government of Nepal to assist in preparing the project proposal. Department of National Parks and Wildlife Conservation (DNPWC) and other relevant ministries and departments are also providing support in shaping and implementing the project.

In this regard, a concept proposal has been developed by DNPWC on critical river basins affected flood prone areas and wetlands in Nepal. The proposed project will focus on climate change induced flooding in critical river basins of Nepal. A technical team, working under the leadership of DNPWC, has identified six critical river basins where youth groups have been actively involved in identifying issues and challenges. Consultations have been carried out with local communities, government agencies, non-governmental organizations (NGOs), private sectors and other stakeholders to identify key issues and challenges.

Similarly, vulnerability of Ganga Lakes in the East, Gandaki and Karnali River basins of Nepal has been assessed. The proposed project will focus on the Ganga Lakes in the East, Gandaki and Karnali River basins of Nepal. Internationally recognized global lakes (PGLL) that are also in the flood prone basins, have the potential to be impacted by climate change induced flooding. The proposed project will support the implementation of the selected PGLL. Nepal and developed flood inundation mapping using the latest available data and information on the selected PGLL. The project will also support the development of a comprehensive plan for the management of the selected PGLL, including the identification of key issues and challenges.

The detailed issues of the project will vary as per watersheds/floodplains/river basins in each critical river basin. The proposed project will focus on the following components: (i) Support to the development of a comprehensive plan for flood risk reduction with a focus on Local Government, (ii) Monitoring Station for flood early warning system and (iii) Support to the implementation of climate change adaptation initiatives including flood mitigation/river restoration and climate resilient livelihood promotion.

The GoN recognizes the importance of proper coordination in terms of project design and implementation. # Gender-responsive adaptation measures

Gender-responsive adaptation measures, utilising a detailed gender-responsive multi-based management and plot to communicate the great gender and reach inequities.

## 1. Introduction

The introduction of the gender and inclusive measures were taken out:

- Overall differential impact of climate change and disaster risks and existing coping mechanisms, and adaptive capacity on men and women in general, children, elderly, persons with disabilities, indigenous peoples and local communities.
- Impact of the climate-related development policies and community-based adaptation measures on the lives and livelihoods of men and women and will particular emphasis of most vulnerable and marginalised groups;
- Risk of women and marginalized groups in contributing towards gender and inclusion of gender-responsive adaptation measures to be addressed to strengthen and foster multistakeholder gender and inclusive climate change adaptation.
- Overall impact of the gender and inclusive measures if any that makes them responsive to the climate changes.

## 2. Approaches and methodology

Within this context, the following principles of the analytical framework guided the data collection, analysis, and interpretation:

- A two-gendered approach was adopted to reflect and analyse the data.

# B. Community level (need demand gap)

At the community level, the focus was on assessing the needs of women and men of diverse socio-economic groups. Their engagement for non-experimental and action research was extended to the community. Some of the key issues identified were: domestic violence, sexual harassment, lack of access to credit, poor health care facilities, and the menace of alcoholism. The community-based approach helped in understanding the needs and aspirations of women and men, their capacities to deal with the impacts of climate change, hazards and disasters. The community-based approach also facilitated the identification of local resources for disaster risk reduction (DRR) and climate change adaptation. This was based on their gender, including considerations of interacting categories of identity such as age, caste, ethnicity, religion, disability, etc. These experiences and understandings were synthesized from ten communities, via a historical review of community practices, and by the synthesis of narratives. Women's groups were also engaged in the process.

# 2.2 Data collection and analysis methods

Convergent and participatory approaches were used to engage a wide and inclusive range of women's collectives in the data collection process. This included:

- **Technical Dimension**
  - Policies and Actions
    The guiding policy Act, policies and actions that affect women's access to resources, M&F systems for gender mainstreaming, control over land, labour and production and marketing.
  - Needs and Priorities
    The needs and priorities of the women are identified and reflected in the action plans.
  - Resources and Opportunities
    The opportunities and resources available to women are identified and reflected in the action plans.

- **Organisational Framework**
  - Technical and Resource Requirements
    The technical and resource requirements for implementing the action plans are identified and reflected in the action plans.
  - Implementation Systems
    The systems that will be used to implement the action plans are identified and reflected in the action plans.

- **Expertise**
  The knowledge of staff and the management personnel is used to support, such as the use of gender analysis tools, gender responsive planning and budgeting, and other relevant resources.

- Government of Nepal's Climate Change Policy 2020
- Approved GC Gender Mainstreaming and Action Plan of other countries such as India.
- Updated Gender Policy and Action Plan (2019, 2023)
- GCF/UN Women's practical manual to support the integration of gender equality in climate change.

## 2.4 Field Data Collection

Following steps were followed to conduct the field data collection:

- Preparatory consultative meetings were held with UNICEF CO-Nepal, their government partners (UNFPA & Regional Office) and with the regional development team members from the Ministry of Forest and Soil Conservation (MoFSC), local governments, community forest user groups, and community-based organizations to identify key informants and community level informants.

  - **KIs and NGOs** were connected with selected individuals that represented various interest groups based on gender, caste/ethnicity, marital status, disabilities, geographic locations, etc. The aim was to conduct a "deep-dive" that would focus on generating clearer understanding of the issues faced by these groups. This included the use of participatory tools and methods which may not be relevant in a group setting.
  - **FGDs were conducted** with different stakeholder interest groups based on gender, caste/ethnicity, marital status, disability, geographical location within the districts. Women's groups, youth groups, persons with disabilities, and other marginalized groups were also consulted. Different participatory tools, such as the use of focus group discussions (FGDs), were used to facilitate the discussions.
  - **Participatory observations** were conducted by the consultant by other stays in the respective villages by spending substantial amounts of time in the village during the implementation phase.

## 2.5 Data analysis, drafting and report finalization

The consultant developed the final GED assistant report based on the analysis of the data collected from the interviews/workshops and other consultations. The finalization of the report was based on the following deliberations:

1. **Final Draft Report:** A detailed draft report was drafted by the consultant.
2. **Participatory GED Action Plan development workshop:** One-day workshops were conducted with community members, LGs, government officials, and NGOs to finalize the GED Action Plan.

# Findings from desk reviews

## 3.1. Status of women in Nepal

### HDI and Gender Inequality Index (GII)

- **HDI**: The Human Development Index (HDI) speaks of a gender disparity index value of 0.847, which is below the national average of 0.569.
- **GII**: The GII of women is estimated to be at least 1.3 years.

According to the Central Bureau of Statistics, Government of Nepal[^2], the total national population in 2011 was 28.94 million with a sex ratio of 95.1% (the national census 2011).

### Socio-economic status

- **Literacy**: The female literacy rate is 63.1%, which is increased to 71.5% in urban areas.
- **Employment**: Only 28.5% of women were employed in agriculture and non-agriculture sectors compared to 48.9% and 83.3% respectively for men.
- **Labor Force Participation**: Women's participation in the labor force has increased from 76.1% in 2001 to 80.5% in 2011, whereas men's participation has decreased from 94.3% to 89.4%.

### Maternal and reproductive health

- **Maternal Mortality Ratio (MMR)**: The MMR is estimated at 259 per 100,000 live births.
- **Contraceptive Prevalence Rate**: The contraceptive prevalence rate has increased from 36% in 2001 to 48.7% in 2011.

### Education

- **School Attendance**: There has been only slight progress in the education sector for women compared to men, with an increase of 5.9 percentage points.
- **Higher Education**: The number of women enrolling in higher education has increased from 38.7% in 2011 to 47.6%.

### Political participation

- **Local Level**: Women's participation at the local level is 34.3%, National Election Commission was 9.3%.
- **Parliamentary Representation**: Women's representation in parliament is only 8.5%.

According to a UN Women report of February 2015, Nepal has made significant progress in women's rights and gender equality over the past decade.

### Violence against women

- **Prevalence**: A national study conducted by the Ministry of Health and Population (MoHP) and UNFPA in 2014 found that 37.6% of ever-married women reported experiencing physical or sexual violence from their husbands.
- **Adolescent Girls**: The adolescent birth rate is 58.2 per 1,000 population aged 15–19 years.

### References

- [HDI Human Development Reports 2014](https://hdr.undp.org/en/2014-report)
- [Gender Inequality Index Data](http://hdr.undp.org/en/content/gender-inequality-index-gii)
- [World Bank Open Data](https://data.worldbank.org/indicator/SI.POV.NAHC?locations=NP)
- [UN Women Nepal](https://nepal.unwomen.org/en)

reproductive age (aged 35-49 years) often face barriers with respect to their usual sources of information and support for decision-making about family planning. Studies have shown that women in this age group are more likely to have had their first live birth at a younger age, have fewer children, and be less likely to use modern methods of contraception compared to older women. In addition, they may face barriers such as lack of access to health care services, limited knowledge about family planning methods, and cultural norms that discourage the use of modern methods.

Work: Key Labor Market Indicators (such as earnings, paid sick leave, and skills in information technology) are important factors influencing women's decisions to have children. These indicators also affect their ability to balance work and family responsibilities, including child care and elder care. Additionally, these indicators can influence a woman's decision to delay or avoid pregnancy altogether.

Beverly, women's access to assets including land, physical and financial resources, and social networks is critical for their empowerment and well-being. Access to education, health care, and economic opportunities can also enhance women's ability to make informed decisions about their reproductive health and family planning.

3.2 Gender and Induction of Direct Change Induced Disaster

There is a dearth of quantitative data on variations in direct change due to induced disasters. However, qualitative studies like the National Climate Assessment (NCA) indicate that gender differences exist in the impact of climate change-induced disasters. For example, women are more likely to be affected by floods and landslides than men. This is because women often have less access to resources such as land, housing, and social networks, which can make them more vulnerable to the impacts of natural disasters.

The study further showed that women's vulnerability was exacerbated by their lack of access to information and services related to disaster risk reduction. Women were also found to be more likely to be affected by floods and landslides than men. # Nepal Earthquake 2015: See the figures below

Assessments and studies carried out in terms of earthquakes and disasters have shown that women are more vulnerable to disasters than men. In the case of the April 25th earthquake in Nepal, earthquake, lack of gender sensitivity in post-disaster relief, improved understanding of the existing health working systems in Nepal, and need to make early warnings systems effective are some of the issues that need to be addressed. The earthquake has also indicated an increased risk of morbidity among women in particular. In the case of differential impacts of floods or landslides, it is important to understand the gendered nature of these disasters and their impact on women's health. The National Federation of Disabled - Nepal (NFDN) also claims that people with disabilities are more vulnerable to disasters.

One of the three studies is a preparation of the GCF project commissioned to ICMOD in UNDP has produced an inventory of the PDS with estimates based on the population census of 2011. The study was conducted by the Department of National Parks and Wildlife Conservation (DNPWC) in collaboration with the Institute for Social and Environmental Research and several international partners. The maximum number of people affected by the flood is estimated to be between 15,000 in Saptari and 33,682 in Bara. Gender-wise, the study found that women are more vulnerable than men. The study also found that the most affected age group is 15-49 years with a population of 74,544 households, of which 62,493 are female-headed and 82,088 are male-headed.

### 1.3 Gender and Induction

According to a recent study by London School of Economics and Political Science, University of Cambridge and the World Bank, women's participation in disaster response is crucial for effective recovery. The study found that women's involvement in decision-making processes during a disaster "is to be noted in the context of Nepal, during the 2006 Gorkha earthquake, when the affected population was mostly female and the community-based relief efforts were led by women." The study also highlighted the importance of women's participation in post-disaster reconstruction and recovery. It is evident that in any disaster, disproportionately large numbers of women particularly from marginalized communities are affected. This has been established that women provide care to their families and are more vulnerable during disasters. They are also more likely to suffer from mental health issues such as depression and anxiety. The study found that women are not adequately represented in the decision-making process and are often excluded from post-disaster reconstruction efforts. It is important to note that women's participation in disaster response is essential for effective recovery and sustainability of communities.

Blindness amongst women with disabilities (PWD) are also more vulnerable by CC-related diseases.

Women are often the first to be affected by disasters and the last to recover from them in disaster situations. All other factors on people who live become impoverished through the disaster, neglecting important aspects of women's lives such as pregnancy, childbirth, and menstrual hygiene management. Women need to address the underlying factors causing gender-based vulnerability and exposure to risk.

Early warning systems (EWS) can be an empowering tool for women to make early decisions that save their lives and livelihoods. Women may be empowered to manage their participation in decision-making processes related to disaster risk reduction.

An **ANMOD** Working Paper published in 2014 demonstrates there is a lack of gender equality in post-disaster relief in the study, Kailali District, Nepal. The study shows that women are more vulnerable to disasters and have less access to resources than men. Women face greater risks for women, with the disruption of social support networks and disempowerment in post-disaster situations. Women are often unable to engage in decision-making processes related to disaster risk reduction. Women's participation in community-based disaster management is often ignored.

In another field study, Early Warning Systems in Nepal showed that local communities were not aware of the importance of early warning systems. The study found that women were more likely to be affected by disasters than men. The study also found that women were less likely to have access to information about early warning systems. The study suggested ways to make early warning systems more effective and accessible to women. The study recommended that women should be involved in the development of early warning systems. The study also recommended that women should be trained in the use of early warning systems.

This implementation and demonstration of early warning systems requires building the capacity of local communities in contributing to and benefiting from early warning system processes, including the integration of gender equality into disaster risk reduction strategies. This will help to disseminate the message that women can also take an active role in local decision-making related to disaster risk management.

Furthermore, it is important to recognize that women play an active role in family livelihood security and food production. Women are often the primary caregivers for children and elderly members of their families.

## 3.4 Importance of gender and inclusion in disaster

The importance of integrating GESI in climate change induced disaster is recognized nationally and internationally.

International agreements/strategy/commitments, such as Sendai Framework for DRR 2015-2030, PDNA, Post Disaster Needs Assessment and Recovery, 2019, Sustainable Development Goals at national level, and the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) are some of the international commitments that preach for effective response and to “Build Back Better” in recovery, rehabilitation and reconstruction. These also emphasize on gender equality and promote gender equitable and universally accessible response, recovery, rehabilitation and reconstruction.

It is also important to note that the integration of GESI in climate change induced disaster risk management is recognized by the government of Nepal such as National Climate Change Policy 2019, Nation Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) to Climate Change, National Disaster Management Authority Act 2015, National Disaster Risk Management Strategy 2017 and the National Disaster Risk Management Authority Act 2015. These also emphasize on gender equality and promote gender equitable and universally accessible response, recovery, rehabilitation and reconstruction.

However, there is limited understanding and analysis of what the linkages are between GESI and climate change induced disaster and how to integrate GESI in disaster and climate risk management.

# 3.5 National gender and inclusion policies (DRM reports)

Regional actions gender and inclusion policies and practices in favour for Nepal, the Constitution of August 2015, guarantees a quasi-feminized approach regarding gender equality and social inclusion. The government has been working on the implementation of all activities before the last Special provisions are made for the protection, empowerment or promotion of women, children, Dalits, Janajatis, Madhesis, Muslims, persons with disabilities and other marginalized groups. The government has guaranteed the right to participate in all state mechanisms. Dalits have specific quotas for their representation in the state structures. The government is also working on education with scholarships from primary to higher education and special provisions for health and social security.

In ensuring the constitutional rights, the CEDAW Session Act 2007 has implemented the removal of all forms of discrimination against women and girls. The National Human Rights Commission Act 1991 has been amended in 2007 to include gender equality and social justice. The government has also established a special mechanism for the protection of human rights and the promotion of gender equality and social justice. The government has also established a special mechanism for the protection of human rights and the promotion of gender equality and social justice.

Human rights actions and guidelines recognize the need to address CEDAW (Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women) recommendations and guidelines to achieve gender equality and social justice. All three sectors have been working together with the government, civil society organizations, the private sector, and other stakeholders to promote gender equality and social justice. The government has also established a special mechanism for the protection of human rights and the promotion of gender equality and social justice.

Regarding legislation, guidelines to promote CEDAW and local governments based on the Ministry of Women, Children and Social Welfare have been developed. The government has also developed a GESI policy of 2015 that promotes effective participation of women in all sectors. The government has also developed a GESI policy of 2015 that promotes effective participation of women in all sectors. The government has also developed a GESI policy of 2015 that promotes effective participation of women in all sectors.

## 2.6 Gender frameworks and policies

The GFC has a standard framework for developing a gender equality and inclusion component in its programs. The GFC’s Gender Equality and Social Inclusion Policy (GESI) provides guidelines to ensure that the fund’s gender-responsive programs, policies and action plans. In this regard, the GFC Gender Policy and Action Plan 2019-2023 ensures that:

- Women are well placed to participate in decision-making bodies at all levels.
- Women have access to resources and opportunities for leadership development.
- Climate change impacts women and men differently; to the disadvantage of women, and existing gender inequalities are likely to be exacerbated by climate change.
- Gender inequality exacerbated by climate change is linked, as an other development issue, to vulnerability and risk. The greater vulnerability of women to natural disasters is due to their limited access to resources, information, and labor market opportunities, lower income, and lesser household decision-making power. Women’s access to control over land and property is also limited.
- The GFC’s commitment to gender equality centers on gender-responsive programs and policies that facilitate the empowerment of women and men, and ensure marginalized and excluded groups are represented in decision-making processes.

The GFC’s commitment to gender equality continues by ensuring that all programs and projects are gender responsive. This includes facilitating the development of a gender-responsive climate change policy, and action plans to address gender inequalities in climate funding by mainstreaming gender equality into climate change policies.


---
**Page 179**
---

gender in the project cycle. Finally, it means measuring the outcomes and impacts of project interventions on gender equality. Gender mainstreaming should shape the entire project cycle: from project identification to implementation and evaluation (M&E). Gender mainstreaming is the process of assessing the implications for women and men of planned and actual policies, legislation and programmes in all sectors and at all levels. It is the process of making the interests, concerns and experiences of women as well as men an integral part of the design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of policies and programmes so that both women and men benefit equitably.

### 3.7 Report on consultations for project formulation

In line with the GCF requirements and social policy that observes the commitment of GCF to gender equality and social inclusion, the project team conducted a series of engagement of various stakeholders during project formulation. **Nepal Federation of Indigenous Nationalities (NEFIN)**, **Indigenous Peoples Federation (IPF)** and **National Federation of Indigenous Women (NFIW)** were among the key stakeholders engaged in the consultations. The consultations were held with the objective to understand the needs, concerns and priorities of indigenous peoples. The consultations were conducted through focus group discussions, interviews and surveys. The consultations were held in different locations across the country including Kathmandu, Pokhara, Chitwan, Dang, Surkhet, Jumla, Humla, Mugu, Dolpa, Rukum, Gorkha, Sindhuli, Saptari, Siraha, Dhanusha and Mahottari. The consultations were held with the objective to understand the needs, concerns and priorities of indigenous peoples.

# Findings from Fieldwork

## 4.1 Community level findings

### 1. Who are the most vulnerable to Climate Change induced Disasters and hazards?

- **Gender**: Women are more vulnerable to climate change impacts due to their dependence on natural resources for livelihoods and the most affected by climate change induced disasters and hazards and committed to climate change adaptation. Women are more exposed to environmental risks such as floods, droughts, and other climatic hazards. They are also more likely to be involved in activities that can mitigate and adapt to climate change impacts.
- **Age**: The elderly are particularly vulnerable due to their dependence on natural resources for livelihoods and the most affected by climate change induced disasters and hazards and committed to climate change adaptation. They are also more likely to be involved in activities that can mitigate and adapt to climate change impacts.
- **Health**: People with health issues are more vulnerable to climate change impacts due to their dependence on natural resources for livelihoods and the most affected by climate change induced disasters and hazards and committed to climate change adaptation. They are also more likely to be involved in activities that can mitigate and adapt to climate change impacts.

### 2. What are the main drivers of vulnerability?

- **Livelihoods**: Livelihoods of men and women depend on natural resources such as forests, rivers, and agricultural lands. These livelihoods are affected by climate change and other socio-economic opportunities and threats, making them vulnerable to climate change impacts.
- **Education**: Education levels affect the ability to adapt to climate change. Higher education levels can lead to better understanding of climate change risks and adaptation measures.
- **Health**: Health conditions have affected both their material conditions and social status in society. Hence, health issues remain, understanding the nature of vulnerability in the context of climate change is important. Chronic diseases and disability risks have further exacerbated these conditions thus increasing vulnerability.

### 3. What are the main coping strategies?

- When the flood became too high, people fled their homes from the government and social organizations. People took away livestock, motorcycles and boats were provided. The agencies handled the situation well by providing food and other essentials to the affected families. They also supported one or two free days and extended it. Hence a large family had food and had saved for 5 months.
- **Peer and Highly Marginalized Adults**: People (Designated People) 3. Similarly to the Hill people, the poorest and most marginalized groups are particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts. They often lack access to resources and services that can help them adapt to climate change. These groups may include:
    - **Elderly**: The elderly are more vulnerable due to their dependence on natural resources for livelihoods and the most affected by climate change induced disasters and hazards.
    - **Children**: Children are particularly vulnerable due to their dependence on natural resources for livelihoods and the most affected by climate change induced disasters and hazards.

### 4. What are the main challenges (including People) from the Hill and mountain areas, particularly in terms of vulnerability?

- **Lack of Resources**: Lack of resources such as food, water, and shelter makes them more vulnerable to climate change impacts.
- **Geographical Remoteness**: Their geographical remoteness means that they are less likely to have access to services and support that can help them adapt to climate change.

# Women As Changed by Men

Women across all social and economic groups have been affected by the changes brought about by men. The impact of these changes has varied according to the opportunities offered.

However, the stages of evolution are subtly different among the various groups. For example, the women who work in the fields or in factories have been most affected by the changes. The women who work at home and in the fields have been most affected by the changes. The women who work at home and in the fields have been most affected by the changes.

## Single Women, Elderly Persons with Disability and Pregnant and Lactating Women

These groups, particularly women have problems related to getting food and cooking fuel. They also face difficulties in accessing health care services. They are often deprived of their basic needs such as clean water, adequate housing, and access to sanitation facilities. However, they are often denied the opportunity to participate in decision-making processes at the household level and in community meetings.

### Women As Changed by Men

The changing climate and increase in the magnitude and frequency of natural disasters has caused problems for women available, agricultural, forest resources and increased male migration. This has led to an increase in the burden on women who are responsible for household work, food security and use of income. The program made by women in the community is to improve their living conditions and reduce poverty. Women's decision-making in society also seems to be declining. Due to the shortage of labor force, including a large number of women have been more critical.

### Water Carrying

Women carry water and marginalized women have the hardest. The increasing burden on women has led to an increase in the number of women who are responsible for carrying water. Women spend hours each day collecting water from distant sources. Water scarcity has increased the time spent by women fetching water, which has led to a decrease in their ability to participate in other activities.

Women carry water and marginalized women have the hardest. The increasing burden on women has led to an increase in the number of women who are responsible for carrying water. Women spend hours each day collecting water from distant sources. Water scarcity has increased the time spent by women fetching water, which has led to a decrease in their ability to participate in other activities.

received information about these trainings and whether they consulted during the planning of the actions for them.

- Majority of the banks and credit groups, cooperatives and micro finance institutions have been involved in the training on gender equality and women empowerment. This has helped to increase their awareness and understanding of gender issues and the need to address them. Further, the underlaying steps and mechanisms of the local economic development activities are being implemented by these institutions and address their needs, interests and priorities in lending and planning, despite challenges such as resource gaps and organizational and planning constraints.

### 4.
The participation of women and men in local development planning, credit risk management and decision making activities are higher

- The general perception, difficulties, geographical location, caste/ethnicity, age, economic status, literacy and ability of the local women and men plays a major role on, who participates in these trainings. Women from marginalized groups such as Dalits and Adivasis and those with disabilities are often excluded from participation in these trainings due to lack of resources and types of barriers that can be categorized as physical, social, cultural and economic.

- Majority of poor and highly marginalized women, particularly from remote and disaster prone locations are not members of local development groups or decision making bodies or have limited access to information. This is because they do not have the capacity to participate in these trainings due to lack of resources. These categories of women are often excluded from the planning process with the poor and marginalized men, who are also excluded from the same. Many's participation in local development activities is limited by their inability to contribute to labour contributions.

- Women in the leadership positions are members of key committees, but they are not always able to participate effectively due to lack of time, health, agriculture (fields) distribution, conflict resolution - violence against women etc. Further, most of the issues during these meetings should be resolved between technical and local level staff. This is a major challenge for women who have been perceived as a threat to the local power centers. A broad and selecting or excluding of women from the leadership positions can also make their participation more limited with no influence in the planning processes and decision making.

- Mostly "Male Champions" are engaged in the activities and only related to large scale issues. Additional attention should be given to address the needs of women, who are mostly new to leadership positions affected by social discrimination, lack of resources and lack of support from their families. They also face challenges due to lack of time and resources.

# Workers were found to be engaged in three domains

During the entire fieldwork, only a few women were seen working outside their homes. Women were actively engaged in the work of planning and cooking meals.

> "Women like us, who can read, make demands and question the system are not the preferred lot by the local government and their technical committees (such as food, irrigation, electricity). They prefer to work with illiterate women because they are easier to control." - A woman elder, Chaudhar

Overwhelmingly old remittances were absent. Marginalized men actively participated in local leadership positions, and knowledge decisions related to local-level planning and disaster risk reduction was also made by them.

> "It is really off if you just look at the number of women elected or nominated women members (and their participation) in the local government bodies. But if you look at the actual work that they do, then you will see that this problem of inequality is serious." - Amina, RUBAHINI/NGO Central

| Daily Engagement at Community Level | Community Participation (Voluntary/Involuntary) | Examples | Gender | Community Engagement (Voluntary/Involuntary) | Examples | Gender |
|-------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------|----------|--------|---------------------------------------------|----------|--------|
| Farm work                           | Women - mostly household chores and farming | Women - mostly household chores and farming | Men - mostly farming, household chores and community work | Women - mostly household chores and farming | Men - mostly farming, household chores and community work |
| Most poor and marginalized            | Women - mostly household chores and farming | Women - mostly household chores and farming | Men - mostly farming, household chores and community work | Women - mostly household chores and farming | Men - mostly farming, household chores and community work |

At the end of the fieldwork I still do not know if it is fair to think of women as a homogenous group. On the one hand, they are actively engaged in planning and cooking meals for their families. On the other hand, they are also actively involved in local-level planning and disaster risk reduction.

> "Government Women's Network for Natural Resource Management Associations are the voice of districts of Nepal." - A woman elder

Women are also active members of the community of fish farms. They are mostly not leading positions but are actively engaged in the work of fish farming. They are also involved in local-level planning and disaster risk reduction.

### 4.2 Institutional level findings

6. The planning and decision-making processes seem to be largely controlled by the local policies and power, side lining the participatory and inclusive processes and structures established and promoted by the national and development partners.

- Local points and areas (structures) play a major role in influencing which groups or individuals are included in the planning process. This is based on who they know, where they live, what matters to them, who matters, authority, gender and how and who is best placed to make decisions about the community. It also depends on whether women and men who are not part of their networks and their needs, interests and concerns are included in the planning process. This is particularly important during disasters when they tend to get excluded. In the majority of the cases it is the local leaders, elders, community and decision makers that are involved in the planning processes. In some cases, women and youth are also involved. The level of participation depends on the level of trust between the local government and the communities. In some cases, the local government has been known to exclude certain groups from participating in the planning process. This is often due to political decisions, legal requirements, social norms, gender and age differences, which provides a barrier for their inclusion.

“We are not included when they design the whole design to get other the local (cultural) level planning meetings, but only when they want us to do something. They don’t include them in the design process and we are not involved in the planning. And finally, they come and tell us what is going on. We are not included in the planning process for implementing their programs. This is a problem that we face every day.” Male community member, Tura.

* “We have been working with the local government to get the local (cultural) level planning meetings, but only when they want us to do something. They don’t include them in the design process and we are not involved in the planning. And finally, they come and tell us what is going on. We are not included in the planning process for implementing their programs. This is a problem that we face every day.” Male community member, Tura.

* “We have been working with the local government to get the local (cultural) level planning meetings, but only when they want us to do something. They don’t include them in the design process and we are not involved in the planning. And finally, they come and tell us what is going on. We are not included in the planning process for implementing their programs. This is a problem that we face every day.” Male community member, Tura.

# The largest challenge is implementing the resettlement and rehabilitation programmes for the affected people. The government has allocated a budget of Rs. 10 million in the last financial year to assist the families in Puttalam district, where people with disabilities in Naun Bazar and Tura region received four months' pay for working on sub-district level projects near their homes. Since they have provided assistance to the disabled, the government has also allocated funds for the purchase of wheelchairs.

The impact of changing ultimate rights with the transition from the old government system to the new federal system has brought both opportunities and challenges in women's leadership and employment processes. A large number of women have come


---
**Page 190**
---

into leading positions. But due to reasons such as decrease in dedicated human resources and lack of support from higher management, the integration of activities and organised platforms in the process, and weak motivation and support women, it is difficult without training and capacity to take an informed decision.

- In the previous governance systems, the value development committee (VDC) was the main body responsible for planning and decision making. The VDC was a committee of 10 members, with only 2 or 3 being women. The VDC was responsible for the planning and decision processes. Hence, the chairperson and his two deputy chairpersons were all men. This meant that the decisions made by the VDC were not always in line with the needs and interests of the women farmers. Now it is made through the value chains. The women are actively involved in the planning process and decision making at the local level, which has increased their participation and interest. The merging of districts brought the women together with the local government and other stakeholders to increase their capacity for support and services.

“Nowadays it is difficult to even get a packet of seeds without information or new seeds directly from the government. The farmers are not aware of the availability of new varieties of seeds in the market. There is no proper system to provide information on new varieties of seeds. Before, there were some private companies that used to supply seeds and other inputs to the farmers. Nowadays, these companies are not available. The farmers are not aware of the new varieties of seeds.”

- There seems to be awareness among the national and local level institutions about the importance of gender equality in development processes. However, the capacity on how to integrate in the design and planning cycle is limited.

- Knowledge and skills on participatory methods and approaches, gender and social analysis, leading to gender integration; the national and regional institutions such as the National Agricultural Research System (NARS) and the International Crops Research Institute for Semi-Arid Tropics (ICRISAT), have been working with the local research centres and farmer communities. They have been conducting training on gender issues in agriculture and rural development, and have been promoting gender equality in their programmes. However, there is a need to increase the capacity of these institutions to integrate gender into their planning and decision making processes.

- For example, the agricultural research centres in the field with heavy focus on extension services, do not have dedicated staff with expertise on gender and social issues to support the farmers. marginated groups do not fall in these criteria and hence majority of the caregiver families of advanced HIV.

- The poor incentives on developing appropriate and GIS-based inclusive exclusion strategies for the most vulnerable groups are a major concern. This is particularly true for people living with disabilities, who are often excluded from programs that are designed to support them. For example, people with disabilities in many countries are not included in the census or other surveys, making it difficult to identify their needs and develop appropriate interventions. The lack of technical and legal language in the GIS-based exclusion criteria also makes it difficult for people with disabilities to access the services they need.

  "There you go again, a meeting on best practices and the women are mostly women so as usual the men sit back and observe. Almost as could not understand what is being said or how to participate." (Farmer, JPS, Bess, Packium S, Sakkab)

- Besides the lack of incentives, the low-level institutions do not have structures and mechanisms for integrating GIS in their planning cycles, making it difficult to develop appropriate interventions. This is particularly true for women's development offices (WDO) that are often understaffed and lack the capacity to integrate GIS into their planning processes. WDO is now merged with the municipality with only one staff and the separated planning office for gender related activities as not a priority.

- The stakeholders and beneficiaries, particularly in climate disaster risk management, are largely considered as a homogenous group. The historical exclusion of the poor women and highly marginalized groups from decision-making processes has made it difficult to complete their requirements with the advanced groups.

  - The local level disaster risk management committees and district disaster management committees do not have capacity to integrate GIS in their planning cycles. This is particularly true for the most vulnerable groups, such as people living with disabilities, who are often excluded from programs that are designed to support them. For example, people with disabilities in many countries are not included in the census or other surveys, making it difficult to identify their needs and develop appropriate interventions. The lack of technical and legal language in the GIS-based exclusion criteria also makes it difficult for people with disabilities to access the services they need.

    "Disaster risk management is not distinguished between the rich and the poor and women are not have special provisions for the poor and marginalized during disaster response and recovery. This does not include women with disabilities." (Ministry of Disaster Management and Conflict Resolution, Khulna)

**Page 192**

# 5. Key Recommendations

1. Establish a working definition of the primary stakeholders (women, marginalized, highly marginalized and vulnerable groups) for the project area, by constituting and operationalizing an inclusive multi-stakeholder platform that includes representatives from government agencies and civil society organizations.

   - **Historical/Political Exclusion** – engage ethnic groups based on gender, caste, ethnicity, indigenous people (e.g. Dalits, particularly from rural areas), Tamangs with specific focus on women, marginalized and vulnerable groups, and persons with disabilities.
   - **Social/Cultural Vulnerability** due to customs, change and disaster risks; particularly women and recently displaced/migrated families affected by disasters.
   - **Economically Poor Households** (living from food security for 3 months or less [month/year]).
   - **Women and Men (Contribution)** who are directly engaged in the day to day management of agriculture, livestock, fisheries, forestry, water resources and other natural resource-based livelihoods.

2. Identify and promote trusted women-friendly and climate smart technologies and practices that benefit at household and farm levels by investing strongly with specialized extension services and local institutions to ensure adoption and use of these technologies and practices by marginalized women’s scope/needs.

   - The focus of the technology and practice could be on water recharge and management, soil health, crop diversification, climate-smart agriculture, promoted by agencies such as DFIDSC, ICIMOD, JICA, SWITCH-ASIA, etc., appropriate technologies for agricultural production (e.g. CIMMYT, IFPRI), improved seeds, organic farming, agroforestry, and integrated pest management.
   - Promote the use of agricultural products, local foods, climate resilient agriculture and crop-livestock integration to enhance nutrition security and food sovereignty.

3. Strengthen existing or form new women and marginalized people’s groups (e.g. Women, Dalits, Indigenous People, Marginalized Groups) through capacity building on leadership, networking, marketing, financial/agricultural loan products, influence planning and budgeting processes at local levels.
   - Ensure that women and men who are not included nor part of any group are also reached by the project through targeted outreach and mobilization and end-to-end climate compatible planning structures.

Page 9 of 11


---
**Page 193**
---

# Ensure that the GSI-related groups and networks allow both women and men to learn from each other’s experiences and to share best practices.

- Develop technical knowledge and skills of women and marginalized groups to improve their ability to participate in decision-making processes.
- Ensure that financial commitment budgets are also allocated for GESI-related activities including GESI regional/infrastructural setup as safe houses for shelter during disasters, mentoring funds etc.

# Increase poor and marginalized women and men income and employment by hopping them into the value chain of GSI.

- For example, poultry, livestock, vegetable seeds, inputs etc. promoted by ADPC, FAO, IFAD, ILO, UNIDO, UNDP, WFP, World Bank, etc. facilitating to lease/patriarchal tenure land support agribusiness (agro-forestry) eco-friendly farming.
- For example, MSMEs/USAD’s GSAR, Harpen Benih Progo etc.

# Ensure income, transportation/commute, and easy to understand communication and information dissemination for the poor and marginalized groups in the government financial and technical resources.

- For example, information related to operation such as training, planning meeting, technical information (budgets, loan, insurance/reinsurance), etc. included GSI.
- For example, MSMEs/USAD’s GSAR, Harpen Benih Progo etc.

# Support the Monitoring Information System MIS of the project to capture gendered needs and to give the same in selected areas/women/groups to support it. Develop gender-responsive monitoring tools and indicators to monitor the progress, and follow up the publications and other knowledge sharing events at national and community levels.

- For example, by developing a Monitoring Management strategy plan on GSI and Climate Change Resilience (CCR) in Indonesia.
- The monitoring management efforts will help to monitoring and evaluation procedures.

# Develop a strong working GESI in Climate Change Induced Disaster Risk Reduction and Resilience (DRR-RR) and ensure the implementation of GESI in the project.

- The working strategy could be based on the findings and recommendations of the various studies conducted by UNDP for the preparation of the proposal, such as GESI Assessment Report, Gender Equality and Social Inclusion Strategy for different sectors (Forestry, Agriculture, Ministry of Local Development), The.


---
**Page 194**
---

# Working GEI strategy/policy could include the following:

- Developing working definition of the local community and primary stakeholders in the context of disease and improve charge (and engagement) in the process (see IOM report). This should be used to develop a framework for the integration of the project's objectives/activities/programs in design, planning, implementation and evaluation. This could include the development of indicators and measures of success. Set interest/potential words or messages to plant.

- Designated management quota for women and the marginalized groups. For example, at the local level, a designated management quota for women and the marginalized groups should be included in the design (for example, in key decision-making, membership, implementation and evaluation teams). This could include provisions for development-related equipment, training, materials, funds, etc.)

- Systematically integrate GEI in operational and project cycle. For Working GEI: Integrate GEI in all stages of the project cycle management (design, planning, implementation and evaluation).

- The project's aid to partnership/ donor management and project support should be used to promote gender equality and women's empowerment. This could include provisions for the development of indicators and measures of success for local and gender results in planning and delivery.

- Prevail to conduct "Free, Prior and Informed Consent (FPIC)" on decision-making. The FPIC is to establish better participation and consultation of indigenous population in the project cycle management. This could include provisions for the indigenous population's territory, language, rights, and their human 2015 rights. These are pre-requisites for the development of indicators and measures of success for local and gender results in planning and delivery.

- Develop a comprehensive strategy of good practices that is consistent with the modern global context of international human rights law has created provisions and standards to safeguard the way of life, well-being, and culture of indigenous peoples. This includes the development of indicators and measures of success for local and gender results in planning and delivery.

- Develop a capacity building package with adequate resources, for the project's staff and its key and direct partners to enhance GEI integration:

  - Strengthen knowledge and skills of focal level/ resource persons, technicians and managers (including those from the central office) on gender equality and women's empowerment. This includes developing and implementing training programs, materials, and manuals.

  - Develop a comprehensive strategy of good practices that is consistent with the modern global context of international human rights law has created provisions and standards to safeguard the way of life, well-being, and culture of indigenous peoples. This includes the development of indicators and measures of success for local and gender results in planning and delivery.


---
**Page 195**
---

- The focus of the capacity development package should be on strengthening their:
  - Understanding of gender equality concepts and principles;
  - Perspective, planning and M&E, understanding the linkages between GEI and DRM/Gender Change to leading, Organizational Assessment (including accountability);
  - Gender responsive planning and budgeting.
- Develop and implement gender and social inclusion responsive materials, approaches and methods.

# Appendices

## Annex 1: Checklist for data collection

### Guiding Questions for International Level Data Collection (for federal, district and local levels)

- What are the guiding principles of the institutional [GESI/GCED] analysis units?
  - Are these principles clearly defined in a policy or guideline on GESI if so, is this a recent document/attempt to develop a GESI policy?
  - Is there a greater expertise and capacity building targeted for GESI?
- Are any changes made to include the [GESI/GCED] system more greatly friendly to terms of gender equality and social inclusion in the institution itself?
  - How does this greater focus made a critical factor in the selection activities and evaluation processes (if any)?
  - What are the guiding principles of the institutional [GESI/GCED] analysis units?
    - Are these principles clearly defined in a policy or guideline on GESI if so, is this a recent document/attempt to develop a GESI policy?
    - Is there a greater expertise and capacity building targeted for GESI?

### Guiding Questions for Community Level Data Collection

#### Maternal Conditions Related Questions - Institutional and Community Based

- What are the guiding principles of the institutional [GESI/GCED] analysis units?
  - Are these principles clearly defined in a policy or guideline on GESI if so, is this a recent document/attempt to develop a GESI policy?
  - Is there a greater expertise and capacity building targeted for GESI?

#### Engagement and Participation

- What are the guiding principles of the institutional [GESI/GCED] analysis units?
  - Are these principles clearly defined in a policy or guideline on GESI if so, is this a recent document/attempt to develop a GESI policy?
  - Is there a greater expertise and capacity building targeted for GESI?

### Assets

- What are the [GESI/GCED] related resources? (the community level and the national level)
  - Who does not have access to these resources?
    - Why is this so, and what are the barriers that prevent them from accessing these resources?
      - What benefits does the most have the focus, related resources information?
        - How do they access this information related resources information?
          - What are the barriers that prevent them from accessing these resources?
            - Who knows very little, and who knows a lot?

**Resources include: social, financial, human, physical and natural capitals.**

## International Politics
The universal declaration of human rights.

## The International Convention on Civil and Political Rights

## The International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights

## Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW)

### GESI Perspectives
- **GESI interventions**
  - Account protection to the right of women's problems.
  - Possible discrimination against women and men before the law.

- **Possible discriminations**
  - Possible discrimination, and rights equally of women and men regarding to rights recognized by the constitution.

- **Adopted in 1979 by the UN General Assembly, is often described as an international bill of rights for women. Contains a comprehensive overview of women's equality rights.**
  - Convention of the elimination against women and sets up an agenda for national action to such.

### Being Distraction and Reform for Action and Being +10
- **Protected the human rights of women, as defined by international human rights instruments, will only be effective if they are implemented in a manner that is non-discriminatory and effectively enforced, at national legal and practical levels, as well as at an institutional level.**
  - Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) (Article 2.8).

### Gender and the UNDP Gender Equality Policies
- **The human rights of indigenous women are protected by international human rights instruments.**
  - Also call for particular attention to be paid to their situation, including their right to participate in decision-making processes to ensure that indigenous women enjoy the full protection and guarantees against discrimination.

### The Hyogo Framework for Action (HFA)
- **It is the first plan to explain, assess and detail the work that is required from all different actors and stakeholders to reduce disaster risk and losses in a timely and efficient manner.**
  - Agreed on with the many partners needed to reduce disaster risks and many others.
  - Bringing them into a common system of accountability. It does not dismiss the importance of national and local systems for achieving disaster resilience, its goal is to strengthen the contribution of all stakeholders and communities to global efforts in reducing disaster losses.

# UN Convention on Rights of Persons with Disabilities (UNCRPD)

- Adopted in 2007, aims to promote, protect and ensure the full and equal enjoyment of all human rights and fundamental freedoms by all persons with disabilities and to promote respect for their inherent dignity.

## National Policies and Strategies

### Disaster Risk Management Act 2014 (DRMA)

- Have provisions that women at risk of disaster, children, senior citizens, urban poor, migrants, prisoners and people with disabilities be prioritized with special plans and programmes.
- Local government units are directed to include persons with disabilities in their disaster management contingency.

### Gender and Disaster Policy 2018

- Have provisions that women, children, senior citizens, people with disabilities (PWDs), urban poor, migrants, prisoners and other marginalized communities be included in all steps of disaster risk reduction and management.
- Local government units are directed to include persons with disabilities in their disaster management contingency.

## Guidelines: Disaster Risk Reduction Management Plan (DRRMP) 2011

### Guidelines: Disaster Risk Reduction Management Plan (DRRMP) 2011

- Include gender and child caring issues in the development of DRRMP.
- Ensure that the different needs, capacities and contributions of women, children, senior citizens, urban poor, migrants, prisoners and people with disabilities are considered.
- The assistance provided in emergencies is planned and coordinated to ensure that it meets the specific needs of these groups.

### Guidelines: Disaster Risk Reduction Management Plan (DRRMP) 2012

- Include gender and child caring issues in the development of DRRMP.
- Ensure that the different needs, capacities and contributions of women, children, senior citizens, urban poor, migrants, prisoners and people with disabilities are considered.
- The assistance provided in emergencies is planned and coordinated to ensure that it meets the specific needs of these groups.

## Guidelines: Local Disaster Risk Management Plan (LDRRMP) 2013

### Guidelines: Local Disaster Risk Management Plan (LDRRMP) 2013

- Include gender and child caring issues in the development of LDRRMP.
- Ensure that the different needs, capacities and contributions of women, children, senior citizens, urban poor, migrants, prisoners and people with disabilities are considered.
- The assistance provided in emergencies is planned and coordinated to ensure that it meets the specific needs of these groups.

## National Climate Change Policy 2015

### National Climate Change Policy 2015

- Have provisions that women at risk of disaster, children, senior citizens, urban poor, migrants, prisoners and people with disabilities be prioritized with special plans and programmes.
- Local government units are directed to include persons with disabilities in their disaster management contingency.

## Gender Equality and Social Inclusion (GESI) provisions

### Gender Equality and Social Inclusion (GESI) provisions

- Have provisions that women at risk of disaster, children, senior citizens, urban poor, migrants, prisoners and people with disabilities be prioritized with special plans and programmes.
- Local government units are directed to include persons with disabilities in their disaster management contingency.

### Gender Equality and Social Inclusion (GESI) provisions

- Have provisions that women at risk of disaster, children, senior citizens, urban poor, migrants, prisoners and people with disabilities be prioritized with special plans and programmes.
- Local government units are directed to include persons with disabilities in their disaster management contingency.

# National Adaptation Programs of Action (NAPAs) to Climate Change 2010

## Gender Equality and Social Inclusion (GESI) in Local Development 2010

working policies. However, the policy is not informed by a gender equality lens and does not take into account different groups of women and men the context of climate change. The alignment/integration of GESI into the local government planning processes and budgets are not taken into consideration. Even though the policy recognizes that "different vulnerable groups" should be addressed, the policy does not provide specific guidance on how to address these community/livelihoods as a monolithic group or does not take into account the different needs of each group. The policy also does not highlight the differentiated impact of climate change on women and men, and hence the differential impact of GESI on their groups. The policy also does not provide specific guidance on how to mainstream the achievement in the end of the policy period. The policy also does not provide specific guidance on how to monitor the achievement.

### National Disaster Response Framework (NDRF) 2013

The NDRF is a framework that provides guidelines for the protection of women, children, elderly, disabled and other vulnerable groups particularly the vulnerable groups, and extend support to the community affected by disasters. The policy also provides specific guidance on how to address the needs of women, children, elderly, disabled and other vulnerable groups in the humanitarian family planning services, and provide # Forest Sector GESI Strategy

## 2008

- Vision: Achieving full forests and soil conservation is a gender and social equity sensitive and socially inclusive process.
- Objectives:
  - Ensure equitable access to benefits from, and decision-making processes in, forest sector activities for all stakeholders.
  - Outcomes: Equitable policy and institutional frameworks that support gender equality and social inclusion.

## 2016-2025

- Outcome 3 is about inclusive forest sector development.
  - Increase participation, competency and leadership of women, indigenous peoples and other poor and socially excluded groups in forest management and decision-making processes.
  - Outcomes: Equitable access to benefits from forest resources.

## Agriculture Development Strategy (ADS)

### 2015-2025

- Agriculture is one of the main strategies for achieving inclusive growth.
- Although the agriculture sector has significant potential, it remains underutilized.
- ADS aims to promote inclusive agricultural development and ensure that the poorest and most vulnerable groups have access to productive resources where the benefits and land tenure are directly derived from the programme.
- "The direct benefit transfer" is a key strategy for achieving this outcome.

## Fifteenth Plan (2020/7-2030/8)

### Approach Papers

- Its strategies include gender equality and social inclusion mainstreaming in forests, biodiversity and watershed management.
- Working people's states: implementation of GESI strategy and action plan.
